Hi, thanks for seeing me. I suppose I’m here because I’ve been having some trouble keeping my attention where it’s meant to be. It’s not that I’m not trying — I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My mind’s off on three other things, and I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.

Yes… I think so, though I’m a bit reluctant to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I do forget ordinary things more than I’d like to admit.

Like, I’ll mean to reply to a parent’s email and then realize later I never did, or I’ll miss paying a bill until the late notice shows up. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.

I know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.

My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.

Even in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.

I’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I suppose I’m here because I’ve been having some trouble keeping my attention where it’s meant to be. It’s not that I’m not trying — I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My mind’s off on three other things, and I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing to say, especially with my job, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or tap a pen now and then, but I wouldn’t say I feel constantly restless or like I can’t sit still. At least, not in a way that’s really stood out to me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, everyone says something a bit too quickly sometimes, don’t they? But I wouldn’t say I’m generally impulsive or doing risky things without thinking. At least, nothing that’s felt like a big issue to me. Does that sound relevant to what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with organization or time management in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a bit reluctant to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I do forget ordinary things more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to reply to a parent’s email and then realize later I never did, or I’ll miss paying a bill until the late notice shows up. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.\n\nI know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I think I was probably fairly normal in that sense.\n\nI mean, my memory of childhood isn’t perfect, and I suppose parents or teachers might remember it differently, but nothing obvious comes to mind about having loads of energy or not being able to sit still. Does that answer it enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions or have trouble calming down after something upsetting happens?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say that’s a major problem for me, no. I mean, I can get upset like anyone, especially if something stressful happens, but I don’t think I’m losing control of my emotions or anything like that.\n\nI might need a bit of time to cool off sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual to me. Unless you mean something more specific by trouble calming down?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in line or waiting for things you want?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I don’t love waiting in queues, but I don’t think I’m worse than most people with that.\n\nIf I’m waiting for something I want, I might get a bit impatient internally, but I’m not usually interrupting people or pushing ahead or anything like that. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I suppose I’m here because I’ve been having some trouble keeping my attention where it’s meant to be. It’s not that I’m not trying — I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My mind’s off on three other things, and I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing to say, especially with my job, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or tap a pen now and then, but I wouldn’t say I feel constantly restless or like I can’t sit still. At least, not in a way that’s really stood out to me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, everyone says something a bit too quickly sometimes, don’t they? But I wouldn’t say I’m generally impulsive or doing risky things without thinking. At least, nothing that’s felt like a big issue to me. Does that sound relevant to what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with organization or time management in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a bit reluctant to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I do forget ordinary things more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to reply to a parent’s email and then realize later I never did, or I’ll miss paying a bill until the late notice shows up. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.\n\nI know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I think I was probably fairly normal in that sense.\n\nI mean, my memory of childhood isn’t perfect, and I suppose parents or teachers might remember it differently, but nothing obvious comes to mind about having loads of energy or not being able to sit still. Does that answer it enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions or have trouble calming down after something upsetting happens?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say that’s a major problem for me, no. I mean, I can get upset like anyone, especially if something stressful happens, but I don’t think I’m losing control of my emotions or anything like that.\n\nI might need a bit of time to cool off sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual to me. Unless you mean something more specific by trouble calming down?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in line or waiting for things you want?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I don’t love waiting in queues, but I don’t think I’m worse than most people with that.\n\nIf I’m waiting for something I want, I might get a bit impatient internally, but I’m not usually interrupting people or pushing ahead or anything like that. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I mean, I might shift in my chair or tap a pen now and then, but I wouldn’t say I feel constantly restless or like I can’t sit still. At least, not in a way that’s really stood out to me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, everyone says something a bit too quickly sometimes, don’t they? But I wouldn’t say I’m generally impulsive or doing risky things without thinking. At least, nothing that’s felt like a big issue to me. Does that sound relevant to what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with organization or time management in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a bit reluctant to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I do forget ordinary things more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to reply to a parent’s email and then realize later I never did, or I’ll miss paying a bill until the late notice shows up. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.\n\nI know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I think I was probably fairly normal in that sense.\n\nI mean, my memory of childhood isn’t perfect, and I suppose parents or teachers might remember it differently, but nothing obvious comes to mind about having loads of energy or not being able to sit still. Does that answer it enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions or have trouble calming down after something upsetting happens?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say that’s a major problem for me, no. I mean, I can get upset like anyone, especially if something stressful happens, but I don’t think I’m losing control of my emotions or anything like that.\n\nI might need a bit of time to cool off sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual to me. Unless you mean something more specific by trouble calming down?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in line or waiting for things you want?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I don’t love waiting in queues, but I don’t think I’m worse than most people with that.\n\nIf I’m waiting for something I want, I might get a bit impatient internally, but I’m not usually interrupting people or pushing ahead or anything like that. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I wasn’t really one of those kids who was always running around or couldn’t sit still, at least not more than other children as far as I remember.\n\nIf anything, I think people saw me more as a bit daydreamy than hyperactive… but I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Unless you mean something more specific by trouble calming down?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in line or waiting for things you want?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I wasn’t really one of those kids who was always running around or couldn’t sit still, at least not more than other children as far as I remember.\n\nIf anything, I think people saw me more as a bit daydreamy than hyperactive… but I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and completing tasks caused you significant stress or problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried not to make a big deal of it, because I do care and I’m not trying to be unreliable. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I wasn’t really one of those kids who was always running around or couldn’t sit still, at least not more than other children as far as I remember.\n\nIf anything, I think people saw me more as a bit daydreamy than hyperactive… but I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and completing tasks caused you significant stress or problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried not to make a big deal of it, because I do care and I’m not trying to be unreliable. And at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them consistently.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose it’s affecting daily life more than I’d like to admit. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.\n\nI know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. It wasn’t like it suddenly appeared, but since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings, it’s definitely been harder to keep on top of things.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when it started feeling more consistently like a problem rather than just the odd busy week, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been more than six months. I mean, I don’t love waiting in queues, but I don’t think I’m worse than most people with that.\n\nIf I’m waiting for something I want, I might get a bit impatient internally, but I’m not usually interrupting people or pushing ahead or anything like that. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I wasn’t really one of those kids who was always running around or couldn’t sit still, at least not more than other children as far as I remember.\n\nIf anything, I think people saw me more as a bit daydreamy than hyperactive… but I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and completing tasks caused you significant stress or problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried not to make a big deal of it, because I do care and I’m not trying to be unreliable. But it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, the follow-through side has got serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly. And at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them consistently.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose it’s affecting daily life more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties consistently over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been more noticeable over the last… maybe 11 months or so. It wasn’t like it suddenly appeared, but since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings, it’s definitely been harder to keep on top of things.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when it started feeling more consistently like a problem rather than just the odd busy week, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been more than six months. I’d put it at around 11 months where it’s been noticeably worse — since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings.\n\nI mean, I’ve probably had some version of it before, but in terms of it being a consistent problem, yes… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, although I probably would have described it back then as just being a bit careless or disorganized.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like, “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was closely supervising me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d forgotten to turn in.\n\nI suppose I didn’t think of it as an attention issue at the time, but looking back, yes, there were signs pretty early on. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thanks for seeing me. I suppose I’m here because I’ve been having some trouble keeping my attention where it’s meant to be. It’s not that I’m not trying — I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My mind’s off on three other things, and I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing to say, especially with my job, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or tap a pen now and then, but I wouldn’t say I feel constantly restless or like I can’t sit still. At least, not in a way that’s really stood out to me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse or do things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, everyone says something a bit too quickly sometimes, don’t they? But I wouldn’t say I’m generally impulsive or doing risky things without thinking. At least, nothing that’s felt like a big issue to me. Does that sound relevant to what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with organization or time management in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’m a bit reluctant to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I do forget ordinary things more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, I’ll mean to reply to a parent’s email and then realize later I never did, or I’ll miss paying a bill until the late notice shows up. And at home, my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way back, and unless I write it down immediately, there’s a decent chance I’ll come home without it.\n\nI know those probably sound like normal lapses, but it feels like they happen often enough that it’s becoming a bit awkward.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. I think I was probably fairly normal in that sense.\n\nI mean, my memory of childhood isn’t perfect, and I suppose parents or teachers might remember it differently, but nothing obvious comes to mind about having loads of energy or not being able to sit still. Does that answer it enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions or have trouble calming down after something upsetting happens?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say that’s a major problem for me, no. I mean, I can get upset like anyone, especially if something stressful happens, but I don’t think I’m losing control of my emotions or anything like that.\n\nI might need a bit of time to cool off sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual to me. Unless you mean something more specific by trouble calming down?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty waiting for your turn in line or waiting for things you want?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, I don’t love waiting in queues, but I don’t think I’m worse than most people with that.\n\nIf I’m waiting for something I want, I might get a bit impatient internally, but I’m not usually interrupting people or pushing ahead or anything like that. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not doing my job or anything, because I do care and I try hard, but at work I’ve had enough missed follow-through on things that my department chair has put me on a support plan.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, I suppose. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. That’s a bit embarrassing to say, but yes, it does affect both. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though at the time it was mostly framed as me being a bit careless rather than anything serious.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but careless, or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I wasn’t really one of those kids who was always running around or couldn’t sit still, at least not more than other children as far as I remember.\n\nIf anything, I think people saw me more as a bit daydreamy than hyperactive… but I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you’re asking about.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and completing tasks caused you significant stress or problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve tried not to make a big deal of it, because I do care and I’m not trying to be unreliable. But it has caused problems.\n\nAt work, the follow-through side has got serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly. And at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll keep track of them consistently.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose it’s affecting daily life more than I’d like to admit. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties consistently over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been more noticeable over the last… maybe 11 months or so. It wasn’t like it suddenly appeared, but since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings, it’s definitely been harder to keep on top of things.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when it started feeling more consistently like a problem rather than just the odd busy week, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has been more than six months. I’d put it at around 11 months where it’s been noticeably worse — since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings.\n\nI mean, I’ve probably had some version of it before, but in terms of it being a consistent problem, yes… about 11 months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these attention difficulties consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, although I probably would have described it back then as just being a bit careless or disorganized.\n\nEven in primary school, my report cards would say things like, “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was closely supervising me. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d forgotten to turn in.\n\nI suppose I didn’t think of it as an attention issue at the time, but looking back, yes, there were signs pretty early on. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, it’s been noticeably worse for about 11 months now — since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but yes, it’s definitely been going on longer than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention for your entire life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but even in primary school my report cards used to say things like “bright but careless,” or “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.”\n\nAnd my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d forgotten to turn in. At the time it just seemed like I was messy or not trying hard enough, but looking back, it does feel like it’s been there most of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention since before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember exact ages perfectly, but in primary school it was already showing up.\n\nMy report cards used to say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was closely supervising me. And my parents would find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d forgotten to hand in.\n\nAt the time I think everyone just saw it as me being a bit messy or not trying hard enough, but… yes, I’d say it was there before 12.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And my parents would find worksheets all crumpled up in my backpack that I’d forgotten to hand in.\n\nAt the time I think everyone just saw it as me being a bit messy or not trying hard enough, but… yes, I’d say it was there before 12.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant difficulties with attention for at least 6 months, consistently across most areas of life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been noticeably worse for about 11 months, since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added the after-school planning meetings.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but it’s been noticeably worse for about 11 months now, since the school changed the curriculum calendar and added those after-school planning meetings.

So yes, definitely more than six months. I hope that answers what you mean by consistently.

My mind’s off on three other things, and I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s a bit embarrassing to say, especially with my job, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I might shift in my chair or tap a pen now and then, but I wouldn’t say I feel constantly restless or like I can’t sit still. At least, not in a way that’s really stood out to me. I don’t remember being the kind of child who was bouncing off the walls or constantly getting told to sit down. Does that answer it enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your emotions or have trouble calming down after something upsetting happens?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say that’s a major problem for me, no. I mean, I can get upset like anyone, especially if something stressful happens, but I don’t think I’m losing control of my emotions or anything like that.\n\nI might need a bit of time to cool off sometimes, but it doesn’t feel unusual to me. I’d say it’s pretty normal, unless there’s something specific you’re looking for there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed difficulties with focusing or completing tasks in both your work and personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that probably is where it shows up more, actually. My wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she doesn’t really feel she can count on me to keep track of them consistently. And my parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, but just forgotten to hand in.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as what you mean, but looking back it does seem like a similar pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with attention or completing tasks consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, looking back. I mean, I did manage, so it didn’t always seem like a major problem at the time, but even in primary school my reports would say things like “bright but careless,” or that I didn’t finish work unless someone was keeping a close eye on me.\n\nAnd my parents used to find worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or partly done, and just… forgotten to hand in. So yes, I suppose it’s not something that only started recently. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively fidgety or restless when you're in quiet environments, like meetings or when you're reading?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I wouldn’t describe myself as excessively fidgety. I might shift around a bit in a long meeting like anyone would, but I don’t think I’m usually restless in that way.\n\nWith reading, it’s more that my mind can wander, but I’m not sure that’s the same as feeling physically unable to sit still.
